From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is this thing that keeps happening at night. I’ll be in the kitchen, usually just sort of picking at leftovers, and I tell myself I’m only going to have a little bit. But then it’s like part of me is watching it happen and saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and somehow my hand just keeps going back anyway.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I do know. That’s what bothers me. I’m standing there, perfectly aware that I’ve already had enough, and the sensible part of me is trying to put the brakes on, but I don’t seem to listen to it in the moment. That feeling of not being able to stop once I’ve started… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset before these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I’ve tried to think about that because it seems like there should be some clear reason before it happens, like I had a bad day or I’m worried about something, but I can’t always put my finger on it.\n\nSometimes I’m just… up, wandering around the house, and I end up in the kitchen. I wouldn’t say I always notice feeling anxious right beforehand. Maybe a little restless, maybe kind of uncomfortable in my own skin, but I don’t know if that’s stress exactly or just me noticing after the fact and trying to explain it.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed a consistent emotional trigger before it happens. It’s more that once I’ve started eating, then the feeling of being upset with myself comes in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, that’s exactly the part that scares me a little. I’m aware of what I’m doing, but I don’t feel like I’m really in control of it once it gets going.\n\nIt usually starts small, like I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have a bite of leftovers or clean up a little from the fridge, and then I’m standing there late at night with the container open. And there’s this very clear voice in my head, the sensible part of me, saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro. Put it away.” But my hand just keeps going back for more anyway.\n\nSo it’s not like I black out or anything. I know I’m eating. I know I should stop. But in that moment, stopping feels strangely hard, like the decision has already slipped past me somehow. That’s the feeling I mean when I say I don’t feel in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word guilty every single time. Sometimes, yes, I suppose there’s some of that, like this heavy “why did I do that again?” feeling. But I haven’t really sat there and labeled it as guilt, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of the time it’s more like disappointment or frustration with myself. I’ll look at the container or realize how much I’ve eaten, and then I start thinking, “You knew you were going to feel bad about this, so why didn’t you stop?” But whether that’s guilt exactly… I’m not sure. It’s definitely not a good feeling afterward, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat to the point of feeling uncomfortably full during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that does happen. Not to the point where I’m throwing up or anything like that, and I don’t usually feel sick exactly, but there have been nights where I’ve eaten enough that I’m just physically uncomfortable afterward.\n\nIt’s a very specific feeling, like my stomach is stretched and my belt suddenly feels too tight, even if it was fine earlier in the evening. Sometimes I’ll end up going out to my workshop and sitting in that chair for a while, just sort of leaning back and waiting for my stomach to settle because lying down right away feels awful.\n\nAnd while I’m sitting there, I’m very aware that I went past the point where I should have stopped. It’s not just “oh, I had a snack.” It’s more like, “why did I keep going until I felt like this?” So yes, uncomfortably full is probably the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Looking back, I’d say it’s been happening at least once most weeks. It’s not every single night, no, but it’s regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just an occasional thing anymore.\n\nA lot of weeks it’s more like two nights, especially since my cousin moved in. That seems to be when I started noticing it as a pattern, or maybe when it got more consistent. I’ll have a stretch where I think, “Okay, maybe I’ve got a handle on this,” and then another night comes along where I’m back in the kitchen with leftovers again. So… at least weekly, often twice in a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to stop eating once you've started during these episodes, or is it hard to stop even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the main part of it, honestly. It’s not just that I’m hungry and I eat something. It’s more like once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, something gets going and it’s hard to interrupt it.\n\nI’ll have this very clear thought in my head, like, “That’s enough, Alejandro, put it away.” I mean, I know what enough looks like. I’m not confused about that. But then my hand is already going back into the container, or I’m taking another forkful before I’ve really made a decision. It’s like the sensible part of me is standing there watching, telling me to stop, and the rest of me just keeps going anyway.\n\nSometimes I’ll even close the fridge or put the lid back on something, and then a minute later I’m opening it again. So yes, I do want to stop at a certain point, but in the moment it feels much harder than it should. It doesn’t feel like I’m fully in control of it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been tracking it closely enough to say, “Yes, this caused a certain amount of weight gain,” or anything like that.\n\nMy clothes fit a little differently at times, but at my age that can be from a lot of things, and I don’t want to pin it all on the nighttime eating if I don’t really know. I haven’t been getting on the scale regularly, so I’d be guessing.\n\nSo… significant changes? I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed anything major or definite. It’s more that I worry it could be affecting my weight, but I don’t have a clear number or trend to give you.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems with sexual performance or interest around the time these nighttime eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really connected anything like that to the nighttime eating, no. I mean… at 65, some things in that area are not exactly the same as they were when I was younger, but I can’t say there was some clear change that started around the same time as the eating at night.\n\nI haven’t noticed a big drop in interest or a specific performance problem that made me think, “Oh, this is part of the same issue.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking closely, to be honest, but nothing stands out to me as a noticeable change since these episodes have been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your interest in sex since these nighttime eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in any clear way that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, like I said, I’m 65, so I don’t want to pretend my interest in sex is exactly what it was when I was 30 or 40. But if you’re asking whether, when these nighttime eating episodes started, I also noticed my interest in sex dropping off around the same time… no, I can’t really say that happened.\n\nIt’s not something where I thought, “Something has changed here.” Nothing that stands out to me as a definite decrease connected to the eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have less interest in sex than you used to, separate from the nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I suppose, compared with when I was much younger, yes, probably less, but I think that’s more just age and life being different than some sudden change.\n\nIf you mean recently, like in the last few months or around when the nighttime eating became more noticeable, I haven’t really noticed a clear drop. It’s not something I’ve sat down and measured, obviously, and it’s a little awkward to talk about, but nothing has stood out to me as, “My interest is really gone now,” or anything like that.\n\nSo I’d say maybe less than decades ago, sure, but not in a way that feels unusual to me or like a recent problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are related to your interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, no. At least, I haven’t noticed a connection between the two.\n\nI mean, if you’re asking whether I eat at night because of something to do with sex, or because I’m frustrated that way, or whether the eating episodes make me less interested afterward… I can’t say that fits for me. It’s not something I’ve thought about as being linked. The nighttime eating feels like its own thing in my mind, and my interest in sex feels like… well, more separate, I guess.\n\nI suppose there could be things going on underneath that I’m not fully aware of, but from what I can tell, I haven’t noticed my sex drive changing when the nighttime eating is worse or better. Nothing like that has really stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have less interest in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: Compared to when I was younger, yes, I suppose I probably do. I mean, I’m 65, so it would be hard for me to say I have the same level of interest I had decades ago. That wouldn’t really be true.\n\nBut I don’t know that I’d describe it as a big problem or some sudden drop-off. It’s more like… over time, life changes, your body changes, routines change. So yes, less than I used to, if we’re talking broadly. But not something I’ve been especially worried about or something that feels dramatic to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty immediately after one of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “guilty” immediately, exactly. Sometimes afterward I feel a bit disappointed in myself, like, “Why did I do that again?” But it’s not always some big wave of guilt right away.\n\nA lot of the time I’m more just tired, or annoyed, or kind of resigned about it. Then maybe later, if I think back on it, I might feel bad about it. But immediately after? I don’t know… not consistently. It depends on the night, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your lack of control during these nighttime eating episodes is more because of your emotions or because of physical hunger?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more complicated than just physical hunger, because a lot of the time I’m not actually hungry in the normal sense. I’ll have had a perfectly decent dinner with everyone, enough food, not skipping or anything like that, and then later at night I still end up in the kitchen.\n\nIt’s not usually like my stomach is growling and I need food. It’s more that I find myself opening containers, looking in the fridge, tasting a little of one thing and then another thing. Sometimes it almost starts as, “I’ll just have a bite,” and then I’m standing there sampling things even though I already ate. But it definitely often happens when I’m not physically hungry, so the lack of control seems like it’s coming from somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. It’s definitely not a good feeling afterward, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat to the point of feeling uncomfortably full during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that does happen. Not to the point where I’m throwing up or anything like that, and I don’t usually feel sick exactly, but there have been nights where I’ve eaten enough that I’m just physically uncomfortable afterward.\n\nIt’s a very specific feeling, like my stomach is stretched and my belt suddenly feels too tight, even if it was fine earlier in the evening. Sometimes I’ll end up going out to my workshop and sitting in that chair for a while, just sort of leaning back and waiting for my stomach to settle because lying down right away feels awful.\n\nAnd while I’m sitting there, I’m very aware that I went past the point where I should have stopped. It’s not just “oh, I had a snack.” It’s more like, “why did I keep going until I felt like this?” So yes, uncomfortably full is probably the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Looking back, I’d say it’s been happening at least once most weeks. It’s not every single night, no, but it’s regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just an occasional thing anymore.\n\nA lot of weeks it’s more like two nights, especially since my cousin moved in. That seems to be when I started noticing it as a pattern, or maybe when it got more consistent. I’ll have a stretch where I think, “Okay, maybe I’ve got a handle on this,” and then another night comes along where I’m back in the kitchen with leftovers again. So… at least weekly, often twice in a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to stop eating once you've started during these episodes, or is it hard to stop even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the main part of it, honestly. It’s not just that I’m hungry and I eat something. It’s more like once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, something gets going and it’s hard to interrupt it.\n\nI’ll have this very clear thought in my head, like, “That’s enough, Alejandro, put it away.” I mean, I know what enough looks like. I’m not confused about that. But then my hand is already going back into the container, or I’m taking another forkful before I’ve really made a decision. It’s like the sensible part of me is standing there watching, telling me to stop, and the rest of me just keeps going anyway.\n\nSometimes I’ll even close the fridge or put the lid back on something, and then a minute later I’m opening it again. So yes, I do want to stop at a certain point, but in the moment it feels much harder than it should. It doesn’t feel like I’m fully in control of it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been tracking it closely enough to say, “Yes, this caused a certain amount of weight gain,” or anything like that.\n\nMy clothes fit a little differently at times, but at my age that can be from a lot of things, and I don’t want to pin it all on the nighttime eating if I don’t really know. I haven’t been getting on the scale regularly, so I’d be guessing.\n\nSo… significant changes? I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed anything major or definite. It’s more that I worry it could be affecting my weight, but I don’t have a clear number or trend to give you.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems with sexual performance or interest around the time these nighttime eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really connected anything like that to the nighttime eating, no. I mean… at 65, some things in that area are not exactly the same as they were when I was younger, but I can’t say there was some clear change that started around the same time as the eating at night.\n\nI haven’t noticed a big drop in interest or a specific performance problem that made me think, “Oh, this is part of the same issue.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking closely, to be honest, but nothing stands out to me as a noticeable change since these episodes have been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your interest in sex since these nighttime eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in any clear way that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, like I said, I’m 65, so I don’t want to pretend my interest in sex is exactly what it was when I was 30 or 40. But if you’re asking whether, when these nighttime eating episodes started, I also noticed my interest in sex dropping off around the same time… no, I can’t really say that happened.\n\nIt’s not something where I thought, “Something has changed here.” Nothing that stands out to me as a definite decrease connected to the eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have less interest in sex than you used to, separate from the nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I suppose, compared with when I was much younger, yes, probably less, but I think that’s more just age and life being different than some sudden change.\n\nIf you mean recently, like in the last few months or around when the nighttime eating became more noticeable, I haven’t really noticed a clear drop. It’s not something I’ve sat down and measured, obviously, and it’s a little awkward to talk about, but nothing has stood out to me as, “My interest is really gone now,” or anything like that.\n\nSo I’d say maybe less than decades ago, sure, but not in a way that feels unusual to me or like a recent problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are related to your interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, no. At least, I haven’t noticed a connection between the two.\n\nI mean, if you’re asking whether I eat at night because of something to do with sex, or because I’m frustrated that way, or whether the eating episodes make me less interested afterward… I can’t say that fits for me. It’s not something I’ve thought about as being linked. The nighttime eating feels like its own thing in my mind, and my interest in sex feels like… well, more separate, I guess.\n\nI suppose there could be things going on underneath that I’m not fully aware of, but from what I can tell, I haven’t noticed my sex drive changing when the nighttime eating is worse or better. Nothing like that has really stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have less interest in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: Compared to when I was younger, yes, I suppose I probably do. I mean, I’m 65, so it would be hard for me to say I have the same level of interest I had decades ago. That wouldn’t really be true.\n\nBut I don’t know that I’d describe it as a big problem or some sudden drop-off. It’s more like… over time, life changes, your body changes, routines change. So yes, less than I used to, if we’re talking broadly. But not something I’ve been especially worried about or something that feels dramatic to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty immediately after one of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “guilty” immediately, exactly. Sometimes afterward I feel a bit disappointed in myself, like, “Why did I do that again?” But it’s not always some big wave of guilt right away.\n\nA lot of the time I’m more just tired, or annoyed, or kind of resigned about it. Then maybe later, if I think back on it, I might feel bad about it. But immediately after? I don’t know… not consistently. It depends on the night, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your lack of control during these nighttime eating episodes is more because of your emotions or because of physical hunger?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more complicated than just physical hunger, because a lot of the time I’m not actually hungry in the normal sense. I’ll have had a perfectly decent dinner with everyone, enough food, not skipping or anything like that, and then later at night I still end up in the kitchen.\n\nIt’s not usually like my stomach is growling and I need food. It’s more that I find myself opening containers, looking in the fridge, tasting a little of one thing and then another thing. Sometimes it almost starts as, “I’ll just have a bite,” and then I’m standing there sampling things even though I already ate. So if I had to choose, I’d say it doesn’t feel driven by true hunger most of the time.\n\nWhether that means it’s emotions, I don’t know exactly. I’m not always sitting there feeling very upset. But it definitely often happens when I’m not physically hungry, so the lack of control seems like it’s coming from somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. I mean, sometimes I suppose if I’ve had a frustrating day or I’m feeling a bit restless, it might make it more likely, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where I think, “Oh, I was upset, and that’s why it happened.”\n\nA lot of the time it feels more automatic than emotional, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be up, or wandering through the kitchen, or just sort of thinking about having a taste of something, and then it turns into more than I meant it to. It’s not that clear to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I’m retired now, so there are definitely evenings where I’m just sort of… around, you know? The house is quiet, there isn’t some big pressing thing I need to be doing. So I suppose boredom could be part of the setting sometimes.

But it doesn’t feel as simple as, “I have nothing better to do, so I’ll eat.” It’s more like I drift into the kitchen, or I’m up for some reason, and food is there, and then I get caught up in picking at things. Sometimes I could be watching television or reading or doing something else, and it still happens.

So, I don’t know. Maybe having idle time makes it easier to start, but I haven’t really noticed myself thinking, “I’m bored, I’ll eat.” It’s more vague and automatic than that.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is this thing that keeps happening at night. I’ll be in the kitchen, usually just sort of picking at leftovers, and I tell myself I’m only going to have a little bit. But in that moment, stopping feels strangely hard, like the decision has already slipped past me somehow. That’s the feeling I mean when I say I don’t feel in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word guilty every single time. Sometimes, yes, I suppose there’s some of that, like this heavy “why did I do that again?” feeling. But I haven’t really sat there and labeled it as guilt, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of the time it’s more like disappointment or frustration with myself. I’ll look at the container or realize how much I’ve eaten, and then I start thinking, “You knew you were going to feel bad about this, so why didn’t you stop?” But whether that’s guilt exactly… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’ll end up going out to my workshop and sitting in that chair for a while, just sort of leaning back and waiting for my stomach to settle because lying down right away feels awful.\n\nAnd while I’m sitting there, I’m very aware that I went past the point where I should have stopped. It’s not just “oh, I had a snack.” It’s more like, “why did I keep going until I felt like this?” So yes, uncomfortably full is probably the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Looking back, I’d say it’s been happening at least once most weeks. It’s not every single night, no, but it’s regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just an occasional thing anymore.\n\nA lot of weeks it’s more like two nights, especially since my cousin moved in. That seems to be when I started noticing it as a pattern, or maybe when it got more consistent. I’ll have a stretch where I think, “Okay, maybe I’ve got a handle on this,” and then another night comes along where I’m back in the kitchen with leftovers again. So… at least weekly, often twice in a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to stop eating once you've started during these episodes, or is it hard to stop even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the main part of it, honestly. It’s not just that I’m hungry and I eat something. It’s more like once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, something gets going and it’s hard to interrupt it.\n\nI’ll have this very clear thought in my head, like, “That’s enough, Alejandro, put it away.” I mean, I know what enough looks like. I’m not confused about that. But then my hand is already going back into the container, or I’m taking another forkful before I’ve really made a decision. It’s like the sensible part of me is standing there watching, telling me to stop, and the rest of me just keeps going anyway.\n\nSometimes I’ll even close the fridge or put the lid back on something, and then a minute later I’m opening it again. So yes, I do want to stop at a certain point, but in the moment it feels much harder than it should. It doesn’t feel like I’m fully in control of it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been tracking it closely enough to say, “Yes, this caused a certain amount of weight gain,” or anything like that.\n\nMy clothes fit a little differently at times, but at my age that can be from a lot of things, and I don’t want to pin it all on the nighttime eating if I don’t really know. I haven’t been getting on the scale regularly, so I’d be guessing.\n\nSo… significant changes? I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed anything major or definite. It’s more that I worry it could be affecting my weight, but I don’t have a clear number or trend to give you.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems with sexual performance or interest around the time these nighttime eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really connected anything like that to the nighttime eating, no. I mean… at 65, some things in that area are not exactly the same as they were when I was younger, but I can’t say there was some clear change that started around the same time as the eating at night.\n\nI haven’t noticed a big drop in interest or a specific performance problem that made me think, “Oh, this is part of the same issue.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking closely, to be honest, but nothing stands out to me as a noticeable change since these episodes have been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your interest in sex since these nighttime eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in any clear way that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, like I said, I’m 65, so I don’t want to pretend my interest in sex is exactly what it was when I was 30 or 40. But if you’re asking whether, when these nighttime eating episodes started, I also noticed my interest in sex dropping off around the same time… no, I can’t really say that happened.\n\nIt’s not something where I thought, “Something has changed here.” Nothing that stands out to me as a definite decrease connected to the eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have less interest in sex than you used to, separate from the nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I suppose, compared with when I was much younger, yes, probably less, but I think that’s more just age and life being different than some sudden change.\n\nIf you mean recently, like in the last few months or around when the nighttime eating became more noticeable, I haven’t really noticed a clear drop. It’s not something I’ve sat down and measured, obviously, and it’s a little awkward to talk about, but nothing has stood out to me as, “My interest is really gone now,” or anything like that.\n\nSo I’d say maybe less than decades ago, sure, but not in a way that feels unusual to me or like a recent problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are related to your interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, no. At least, I haven’t noticed a connection between the two.\n\nI mean, if you’re asking whether I eat at night because of something to do with sex, or because I’m frustrated that way, or whether the eating episodes make me less interested afterward… I can’t say that fits for me. It’s not something I’ve thought about as being linked. The nighttime eating feels like its own thing in my mind, and my interest in sex feels like… well, more separate, I guess.\n\nI suppose there could be things going on underneath that I’m not fully aware of, but from what I can tell, I haven’t noticed my sex drive changing when the nighttime eating is worse or better. Nothing like that has really stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have less interest in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: Compared to when I was younger, yes, I suppose I probably do. I mean, I’m 65, so it would be hard for me to say I have the same level of interest I had decades ago. That wouldn’t really be true.\n\nBut I don’t know that I’d describe it as a big problem or some sudden drop-off. It’s more like… over time, life changes, your body changes, routines change. So yes, less than I used to, if we’re talking broadly. But not something I’ve been especially worried about or something that feels dramatic to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty immediately after one of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “guilty” immediately, exactly. Sometimes afterward I feel a bit disappointed in myself, like, “Why did I do that again?” But it’s not always some big wave of guilt right away.\n\nA lot of the time I’m more just tired, or annoyed, or kind of resigned about it. Then maybe later, if I think back on it, I might feel bad about it. But immediately after? I don’t know… not consistently. It depends on the night, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your lack of control during these nighttime eating episodes is more because of your emotions or because of physical hunger?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more complicated than just physical hunger, because a lot of the time I’m not actually hungry in the normal sense. I’ll have had a perfectly decent dinner with everyone, enough food, not skipping or anything like that, and then later at night I still end up in the kitchen.\n\nIt’s not usually like my stomach is growling and I need food. It’s more that I find myself opening containers, looking in the fridge, tasting a little of one thing and then another thing. Sometimes it almost starts as, “I’ll just have a bite,” and then I’m standing there sampling things even though I already ate. So if I had to choose, I’d say it doesn’t feel driven by true hunger most of the time.\n\nWhether that means it’s emotions, I don’t know exactly. I’m not always sitting there feeling very upset. But it definitely often happens when I’m not physically hungry, so the lack of control seems like it’s coming from somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. I mean, sometimes I suppose if I’ve had a frustrating day or I’m feeling a bit restless, it might make it more likely, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where I think, “Oh, I was upset, and that’s why it happened.”\n\nA lot of the time it feels more automatic than emotional, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be up, or wandering through the kitchen, or just sort of thinking about having a taste of something, and then it turns into more than I meant it to. But I’m not always aware of feeling sad or angry or anxious right before it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t rule emotions out completely, but I can’t honestly say they’re usually the trigger. It’s not that clear to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating at night because you feel like you have nothing better to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I’m retired now, so there are definitely evenings where I’m just sort of… around, you know? The house is quiet, there isn’t some big pressing thing I need to be doing. So I suppose boredom could be part of the setting sometimes.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel as simple as, “I have nothing better to do, so I’ll eat.” It’s more like I drift into the kitchen, or I’m up for some reason, and food is there, and then I get caught up in picking at things. Sometimes I could be watching television or reading or doing something else, and it still happens.\n\nSo, I don’t know. Maybe having idle time makes it easier to start, but I haven’t really noticed myself thinking, “I’m bored, I’ll eat.” It’s more vague and automatic than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Binge-Eating Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your lack of control during these nighttime eating episodes is more because of your emotions or because of physical hunger?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more complicated than just physical hunger, because a lot of the time I’m not actually hungry in the normal sense. I’ll have had a perfectly decent dinner with everyone, enough food, not skipping or anything like that, and then later at night I still end up in the kitchen.\n\nIt’s not usually like my stomach is growling and I need food. It’s more that I find myself opening containers, looking in the fridge, tasting a little of one thing and then another thing. Sometimes it almost starts as, “I’ll just have a bite,” and then I’m standing there sampling things even though I already ate. So if I had to choose, I’d say it doesn’t feel driven by true hunger most of the time.\n\nWhether that means it’s emotions, I don’t know exactly. I’m not always sitting there feeling very upset. But it definitely often happens when I’m not physically hungry, so the lack of control seems like it’s coming from somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. I mean, sometimes I suppose if I’ve had a frustrating day or I’m feeling a bit restless, it might make it more likely, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where I think, “Oh, I was upset, and that’s why it happened.”\n\nA lot of the time it feels more automatic than emotional, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be up, or wandering through the kitchen, or just sort of thinking about having a taste of something, and then it turns into more than I meant it to. But I’m not always aware of feeling sad or angry or anxious right before it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t rule emotions out completely, but I can’t honestly say they’re usually the trigger. It’s not that clear to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating at night because you feel like you have nothing better to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I’m retired now, so there are definitely evenings where I’m just sort of… around, you know? The house is quiet, there isn’t some big pressing thing I need to be doing. So I suppose boredom could be part of the setting sometimes.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel as simple as, “I have nothing better to do, so I’ll eat.” It’s more like I drift into the kitchen, or I’m up for some reason, and food is there, and then I get caught up in picking at things. Sometimes I could be watching television or reading or doing something else, and it still happens.\n\nSo, I don’t know. Maybe having idle time makes it easier to start, but I haven’t really noticed myself thinking, “I’m bored, I’ll eat.” It’s more vague and automatic than that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself eating at night simply because you're not doing anything else?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess I’d say… not simply because I’m not doing anything else, no. I mean, there are nights where I’m not doing much, sure. At my age and being retired, the evenings can stretch out a bit, and I’m not rushing around like I used to. So I can see how that might make it easier for me to wander into the kitchen.

But it doesn’t feel like I’m sitting there thinking, “Well, I have nothing to do, so I’ll eat.” It’s more slippery than that. I might be between things, or the TV is on, or I’m just puttering around, and then I notice food, or remember something in the fridge, and it kind of starts from there.

So… maybe the empty time gives it room to happen, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t say that’s the reason in a clear, simple way. I haven’t really pinned it down like that.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is this thing that keeps happening at night. I’ll be in the kitchen, usually just sort of picking at leftovers, and I tell myself I’m only going to have a little bit. But then it’s like part of me is watching it happen and saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and somehow my hand just keeps going back anyway.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I do know. That’s what bothers me. I’m standing there, perfectly aware that I’ve already had enough, and the sensible part of me is trying to put the brakes on, but I don’t seem to listen to it in the moment. That feeling of not being able to stop once I’ve started… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset before these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I’ve tried to think about that because it seems like there should be some clear reason before it happens, like I had a bad day or I’m worried about something, but I can’t always put my finger on it.\n\nSometimes I’m just… up, wandering around the house, and I end up in the kitchen. I wouldn’t say I always notice feeling anxious right beforehand. Maybe a little restless, maybe kind of uncomfortable in my own skin, but I don’t know if that’s stress exactly or just me noticing after the fact and trying to explain it.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed a consistent emotional trigger before it happens. It’s more that once I’ve started eating, then the feeling of being upset with myself comes in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, that’s exactly the part that scares me a little. I’m aware of what I’m doing, but I don’t feel like I’m really in control of it once it gets going.\n\nIt usually starts small, like I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have a bite of leftovers or clean up a little from the fridge, and then I’m standing there late at night with the container open. And there’s this very clear voice in my head, the sensible part of me, saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro. Put it away.” But my hand just keeps going back for more anyway.\n\nSo it’s not like I black out or anything. I know I’m eating. I know I should stop. But in that moment, stopping feels strangely hard, like the decision has already slipped past me somehow. That’s the feeling I mean when I say I don’t feel in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word guilty every single time. Sometimes, yes, I suppose there’s some of that, like this heavy “why did I do that again?” feeling. But I haven’t really sat there and labeled it as guilt, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of the time it’s more like disappointment or frustration with myself. I’ll look at the container or realize how much I’ve eaten, and then I start thinking, “You knew you were going to feel bad about this, so why didn’t you stop?” But whether that’s guilt exactly… I’m not sure. It’s definitely not a good feeling afterward, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat to the point of feeling uncomfortably full during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that does happen. Not to the point where I’m throwing up or anything like that, and I don’t usually feel sick exactly, but there have been nights where I’ve eaten enough that I’m just physically uncomfortable afterward.\n\nIt’s a very specific feeling, like my stomach is stretched and my belt suddenly feels too tight, even if it was fine earlier in the evening. Sometimes I’ll end up going out to my workshop and sitting in that chair for a while, just sort of leaning back and waiting for my stomach to settle because lying down right away feels awful.\n\nAnd while I’m sitting there, I’m very aware that I went past the point where I should have stopped. It’s not just “oh, I had a snack.” It’s more like, “why did I keep going until I felt like this?” So yes, uncomfortably full is probably the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Looking back, I’d say it’s been happening at least once most weeks. It’s not every single night, no, but it’s regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just an occasional thing anymore.\n\nA lot of weeks it’s more like two nights, especially since my cousin moved in. That seems to be when I started noticing it as a pattern, or maybe when it got more consistent. I’ll have a stretch where I think, “Okay, maybe I’ve got a handle on this,” and then another night comes along where I’m back in the kitchen with leftovers again. So… at least weekly, often twice in a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to stop eating once you've started during these episodes, or is it hard to stop even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the main part of it, honestly. It’s not just that I’m hungry and I eat something. It’s more like once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, something gets going and it’s hard to interrupt it.\n\nI’ll have this very clear thought in my head, like, “That’s enough, Alejandro, put it away.” I mean, I know what enough looks like. I’m not confused about that. But then my hand is already going back into the container, or I’m taking another forkful before I’ve really made a decision. It’s like the sensible part of me is standing there watching, telling me to stop, and the rest of me just keeps going anyway.\n\nSometimes I’ll even close the fridge or put the lid back on something, and then a minute later I’m opening it again. So yes, I do want to stop at a certain point, but in the moment it feels much harder than it should. It doesn’t feel like I’m fully in control of it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been tracking it closely enough to say, “Yes, this caused a certain amount of weight gain,” or anything like that.\n\nMy clothes fit a little differently at times, but at my age that can be from a lot of things, and I don’t want to pin it all on the nighttime eating if I don’t really know. I haven’t been getting on the scale regularly, so I’d be guessing.\n\nSo… significant changes? I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed anything major or definite. It’s more that I worry it could be affecting my weight, but I don’t have a clear number or trend to give you.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems with sexual performance or interest around the time these nighttime eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really connected anything like that to the nighttime eating, no. I mean… at 65, some things in that area are not exactly the same as they were when I was younger, but I can’t say there was some clear change that started around the same time as the eating at night.\n\nI haven’t noticed a big drop in interest or a specific performance problem that made me think, “Oh, this is part of the same issue.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking closely, to be honest, but nothing stands out to me as a noticeable change since these episodes have been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your interest in sex since these nighttime eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in any clear way that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, like I said, I’m 65, so I don’t want to pretend my interest in sex is exactly what it was when I was 30 or 40. But if you’re asking whether, when these nighttime eating episodes started, I also noticed my interest in sex dropping off around the same time… no, I can’t really say that happened.\n\nIt’s not something where I thought, “Something has changed here.” Nothing that stands out to me as a definite decrease connected to the eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have less interest in sex than you used to, separate from the nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I suppose, compared with when I was much younger, yes, probably less, but I think that’s more just age and life being different than some sudden change.\n\nIf you mean recently, like in the last few months or around when the nighttime eating became more noticeable, I haven’t really noticed a clear drop. It’s not something I’ve sat down and measured, obviously, and it’s a little awkward to talk about, but nothing has stood out to me as, “My interest is really gone now,” or anything like that.\n\nSo I’d say maybe less than decades ago, sure, but not in a way that feels unusual to me or like a recent problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are related to your interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, no. At least, I haven’t noticed a connection between the two.\n\nI mean, if you’re asking whether I eat at night because of something to do with sex, or because I’m frustrated that way, or whether the eating episodes make me less interested afterward… I can’t say that fits for me. It’s not something I’ve thought about as being linked. The nighttime eating feels like its own thing in my mind, and my interest in sex feels like… well, more separate, I guess.\n\nI suppose there could be things going on underneath that I’m not fully aware of, but from what I can tell, I haven’t noticed my sex drive changing when the nighttime eating is worse or better. Nothing like that has really stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have less interest in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: Compared to when I was younger, yes, I suppose I probably do. I mean, I’m 65, so it would be hard for me to say I have the same level of interest I had decades ago. That wouldn’t really be true.\n\nBut I don’t know that I’d describe it as a big problem or some sudden drop-off. It’s more like… over time, life changes, your body changes, routines change. So yes, less than I used to, if we’re talking broadly. But not something I’ve been especially worried about or something that feels dramatic to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty immediately after one of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “guilty” immediately, exactly. Sometimes afterward I feel a bit disappointed in myself, like, “Why did I do that again?” But it’s not always some big wave of guilt right away.\n\nA lot of the time I’m more just tired, or annoyed, or kind of resigned about it. Then maybe later, if I think back on it, I might feel bad about it. But immediately after? I don’t know… not consistently. It depends on the night, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your lack of control during these nighttime eating episodes is more because of your emotions or because of physical hunger?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more complicated than just physical hunger, because a lot of the time I’m not actually hungry in the normal sense. I’ll have had a perfectly decent dinner with everyone, enough food, not skipping or anything like that, and then later at night I still end up in the kitchen.\n\nIt’s not usually like my stomach is growling and I need food. It’s more that I find myself opening containers, looking in the fridge, tasting a little of one thing and then another thing. But it definitely often happens when I’m not physically hungry, so the lack of control seems like it’s coming from somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. But if you’re asking whether, when these nighttime eating episodes started, I also noticed my interest in sex dropping off around the same time… no, I can’t really say that happened.\n\nIt’s not something where I thought, “Something has changed here.” Nothing that stands out to me as a definite decrease connected to the eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have less interest in sex than you used to, separate from the nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I suppose, compared with when I was much younger, yes, probably less, but I think that’s more just age and life being different than some sudden change.\n\nIf you mean recently, like in the last few months or around when the nighttime eating became more noticeable, I haven’t really noticed a clear drop. It’s not something I’ve sat down and measured, obviously, and it’s a little awkward to talk about, but nothing has stood out to me as, “My interest is really gone now,” or anything like that.\n\nSo I’d say maybe less than decades ago, sure, but not in a way that feels unusual to me or like a recent problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are related to your interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, no. At least, I haven’t noticed a connection between the two.\n\nI mean, if you’re asking whether I eat at night because of something to do with sex, or because I’m frustrated that way, or whether the eating episodes make me less interested afterward… I can’t say that fits for me. It’s not something I’ve thought about as being linked. The nighttime eating feels like its own thing in my mind, and my interest in sex feels like… well, more separate, I guess.\n\nI suppose there could be things going on underneath that I’m not fully aware of, but from what I can tell, I haven’t noticed my sex drive changing when the nighttime eating is worse or better. Nothing like that has really stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have less interest in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: Compared to when I was younger, yes, I suppose I probably do. I mean, I’m 65, so it would be hard for me to say I have the same level of interest I had decades ago. That wouldn’t really be true.\n\nBut I don’t know that I’d describe it as a big problem or some sudden drop-off. It’s more like… over time, life changes, your body changes, routines change. So yes, less than I used to, if we’re talking broadly. But not something I’ve been especially worried about or something that feels dramatic to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty immediately after one of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “guilty” immediately, exactly. Sometimes afterward I feel a bit disappointed in myself, like, “Why did I do that again?” But it’s not always some big wave of guilt right away.\n\nA lot of the time I’m more just tired, or annoyed, or kind of resigned about it. Then maybe later, if I think back on it, I might feel bad about it. But immediately after? I don’t know… not consistently. It depends on the night, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your lack of control during these nighttime eating episodes is more because of your emotions or because of physical hunger?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more complicated than just physical hunger, because a lot of the time I’m not actually hungry in the normal sense. I’ll have had a perfectly decent dinner with everyone, enough food, not skipping or anything like that, and then later at night I still end up in the kitchen.\n\nIt’s not usually like my stomach is growling and I need food. It’s more that I find myself opening containers, looking in the fridge, tasting a little of one thing and then another thing. Sometimes it almost starts as, “I’ll just have a bite,” and then I’m standing there sampling things even though I already ate. So if I had to choose, I’d say it doesn’t feel driven by true hunger most of the time.\n\nWhether that means it’s emotions, I don’t know exactly. I’m not always sitting there feeling very upset. I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, food tastes good, obviously, and there are moments where I suppose I do get some pleasure from it—especially right at the start, if it’s something sweet or something I like. I wouldn’t say I’m completely unaware of the taste.

I might notice the first few bites, and then after that it’s almost like I’m just continuing, not really savoring it. Sometimes I’m standing there in the kitchen rather than properly sitting down, so it doesn’t have that feeling of enjoyment exactly.

So… yes and no, I guess. There’s some taste and pleasure in the beginning, but I’m not sure enjoyment is the main thing that’s going on. It’s more like I get started and then I don’t pay as much attention to whether I’m enjoying it.

Sometimes I’ll end up going out to my workshop and sitting in that chair for a while, just sort of leaning back and waiting for my stomach to settle because lying down right away feels awful.\n\nAnd while I’m sitting there, I’m very aware that I went past the point where I should have stopped. It’s not just “oh, I had a snack.” It’s more like, “why did I keep going until I felt like this?” So yes, uncomfortably full is probably the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Looking back, I’d say it’s been happening at least once most weeks. It’s not every single night, no, but it’s regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just an occasional thing anymore.\n\nA lot of weeks it’s more like two nights, especially since my cousin moved in. That seems to be when I started noticing it as a pattern, or maybe when it got more consistent. I’ll have a stretch where I think, “Okay, maybe I’ve got a handle on this,” and then another night comes along where I’m back in the kitchen with leftovers again. So… at least weekly, often twice in a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to stop eating once you've started during these episodes, or is it hard to stop even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the main part of it, honestly. It’s not just that I’m hungry and I eat something. It’s more like once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, something gets going and it’s hard to interrupt it.\n\nI’ll have this very clear thought in my head, like, “That’s enough, Alejandro, put it away.” I mean, I know what enough looks like. I’m not confused about that. But then my hand is already going back into the container, or I’m taking another forkful before I’ve really made a decision. It’s like the sensible part of me is standing there watching, telling me to stop, and the rest of me just keeps going anyway.\n\nSometimes I’ll even close the fridge or put the lid back on something, and then a minute later I’m opening it again. So yes, I do want to stop at a certain point, but in the moment it feels much harder than it should. It doesn’t feel like I’m fully in control of it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been tracking it closely enough to say, “Yes, this caused a certain amount of weight gain,” or anything like that.\n\nMy clothes fit a little differently at times, but at my age that can be from a lot of things, and I don’t want to pin it all on the nighttime eating if I don’t really know. I haven’t been getting on the scale regularly, so I’d be guessing.\n\nSo… significant changes? I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed anything major or definite. It’s more that I worry it could be affecting my weight, but I don’t have a clear number or trend to give you.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems with sexual performance or interest around the time these nighttime eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really connected anything like that to the nighttime eating, no. I mean… at 65, some things in that area are not exactly the same as they were when I was younger, but I can’t say there was some clear change that started around the same time as the eating at night.\n\nI haven’t noticed a big drop in interest or a specific performance problem that made me think, “Oh, this is part of the same issue.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking closely, to be honest, but nothing stands out to me as a noticeable change since these episodes have been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your interest in sex since these nighttime eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in any clear way that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, like I said, I’m 65, so I don’t want to pretend my interest in sex is exactly what it was when I was 30 or 40. But if you’re asking whether, when these nighttime eating episodes started, I also noticed my interest in sex dropping off around the same time… no, I can’t really say that happened.\n\nIt’s not something where I thought, “Something has changed here.” Nothing that stands out to me as a definite decrease connected to the eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have less interest in sex than you used to, separate from the nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I suppose, compared with when I was much younger, yes, probably less, but I think that’s more just age and life being different than some sudden change.\n\nIf you mean recently, like in the last few months or around when the nighttime eating became more noticeable, I haven’t really noticed a clear drop. It’s not something I’ve sat down and measured, obviously, and it’s a little awkward to talk about, but nothing has stood out to me as, “My interest is really gone now,” or anything like that.\n\nSo I’d say maybe less than decades ago, sure, but not in a way that feels unusual to me or like a recent problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are related to your interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, no. At least, I haven’t noticed a connection between the two.\n\nI mean, if you’re asking whether I eat at night because of something to do with sex, or because I’m frustrated that way, or whether the eating episodes make me less interested afterward… I can’t say that fits for me. It’s not something I’ve thought about as being linked. The nighttime eating feels like its own thing in my mind, and my interest in sex feels like… well, more separate, I guess.\n\nI suppose there could be things going on underneath that I’m not fully aware of, but from what I can tell, I haven’t noticed my sex drive changing when the nighttime eating is worse or better. Nothing like that has really stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have less interest in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: Compared to when I was younger, yes, I suppose I probably do. I mean, I’m 65, so it would be hard for me to say I have the same level of interest I had decades ago. That wouldn’t really be true.\n\nBut I don’t know that I’d describe it as a big problem or some sudden drop-off. It’s more like… over time, life changes, your body changes, routines change. So yes, less than I used to, if we’re talking broadly. But not something I’ve been especially worried about or something that feels dramatic to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty immediately after one of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “guilty” immediately, exactly. Sometimes afterward I feel a bit disappointed in myself, like, “Why did I do that again?” But it’s not always some big wave of guilt right away.\n\nA lot of the time I’m more just tired, or annoyed, or kind of resigned about it. Then maybe later, if I think back on it, I might feel bad about it. But immediately after? I don’t know… not consistently. It depends on the night, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your lack of control during these nighttime eating episodes is more because of your emotions or because of physical hunger?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more complicated than just physical hunger, because a lot of the time I’m not actually hungry in the normal sense. I’ll have had a perfectly decent dinner with everyone, enough food, not skipping or anything like that, and then later at night I still end up in the kitchen.\n\nIt’s not usually like my stomach is growling and I need food. It’s more that I find myself opening containers, looking in the fridge, tasting a little of one thing and then another thing. Sometimes it almost starts as, “I’ll just have a bite,” and then I’m standing there sampling things even though I already ate. So if I had to choose, I’d say it doesn’t feel driven by true hunger most of the time.\n\nWhether that means it’s emotions, I don’t know exactly. I’m not always sitting there feeling very upset. But it definitely often happens when I’m not physically hungry, so the lack of control seems like it’s coming from somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. I mean, sometimes I suppose if I’ve had a frustrating day or I’m feeling a bit restless, it might make it more likely, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where I think, “Oh, I was upset, and that’s why it happened.”\n\nA lot of the time it feels more automatic than emotional, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be up, or wandering through the kitchen, or just sort of thinking about having a taste of something, and then it turns into more than I meant it to. But I’m not always aware of feeling sad or angry or anxious right before it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t rule emotions out completely, but I can’t honestly say they’re usually the trigger. It’s not that clear to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating at night because you feel like you have nothing better to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, I’m retired now, so there are definitely evenings where I’m just sort of… around, you know? The house is quiet, there isn’t some big pressing thing I need to be doing. So I suppose boredom could be part of the setting sometimes.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel as simple as, “I have nothing better to do, so I’ll eat.” It’s more like I drift into the kitchen, or I’m up for some reason, and food is there, and then I get caught up in picking at things. Sometimes I could be watching television or reading or doing something else, and it still happens.\n\nSo, I don’t know. Maybe having idle time makes it easier to start, but I haven’t really noticed myself thinking, “I’m bored, I’ll eat.” It’s more vague and automatic than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself eating at night simply because you're not doing anything else?\n\nPatient: I guess I’d say… not simply because I’m not doing anything else, no. I mean, there are nights where I’m not doing much, sure. At my age and being retired, the evenings can stretch out a bit, and I’m not rushing around like I used to. So I can see how that might make it easier for me to wander into the kitchen.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m sitting there thinking, “Well, I have nothing to do, so I’ll eat.” It’s more slippery than that. I might be between things, or the TV is on, or I’m just puttering around, and then I notice food, or remember something in the fridge, and it kind of starts from there.\n\nSo… maybe the empty time gives it room to happen, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t say that’s the reason in a clear, simple way. I haven’t really pinned it down like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the taste or pleasure of eating during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, food tastes good, obviously, and there are moments where I suppose I do get some pleasure from it—especially right at the start, if it’s something sweet or something I like. I wouldn’t say I’m completely unaware of the taste.\n\nBut during those episodes, it’s not like sitting down to enjoy a nice meal, if that’s what you mean. It often feels more hurried or unfocused than pleasurable. I might notice the first few bites, and then after that it’s almost like I’m just continuing, not really savoring it. Sometimes I’m standing there in the kitchen rather than properly sitting down, so it doesn’t have that feeling of enjoyment exactly.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. There’s some taste and pleasure in the beginning, but I’m not sure enjoyment is the main thing that’s going on. It’s more like I get started and then I don’t pay as much attention to whether I’m enjoying it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, there are nights where I’m not doing much, sure. At my age and being retired, the evenings can stretch out a bit, and I’m not rushing around like I used to. So I can see how that might make it easier for me to wander into the kitchen.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m sitting there thinking, “Well, I have nothing to do, so I’ll eat.” It’s more slippery than that. I might be between things, or the TV is on, or I’m just puttering around, and then I notice food, or remember something in the fridge, and it kind of starts from there.\n\nSo… maybe the empty time gives it room to happen, if that makes sense. But I wouldn’t say that’s the reason in a clear, simple way. I haven’t really pinned it down like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the taste or pleasure of eating during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, food tastes good, obviously, and there are moments where I suppose I do get some pleasure from it—especially right at the start, if it’s something sweet or something I like. I wouldn’t say I’m completely unaware of the taste.\n\nBut during those episodes, it’s not like sitting down to enjoy a nice meal, if that’s what you mean. It often feels more hurried or unfocused than pleasurable. I might notice the first few bites, and then after that it’s almost like I’m just continuing, not really savoring it. Sometimes I’m standing there in the kitchen rather than properly sitting down, so it doesn’t have that feeling of enjoyment exactly.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. There’s some taste and pleasure in the beginning, but I’m not sure enjoyment is the main thing that’s going on. It’s more like I get started and then I don’t pay as much attention to whether I’m enjoying it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think there's any connection between your nighttime eating and your level of interest in sex?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

I’m not sure, honestly. That’s not a connection I’ve really thought about before. When the nighttime eating happens, I don’t usually have sex or my interest in sex on my mind, if that makes sense. It feels like a separate thing to me, at least from what I’ve noticed.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is this thing that keeps happening at night. I’ll be in the kitchen, usually just sort of picking at leftovers, and I tell myself I’m only going to have a little bit. But then it’s like part of me is watching it happen and saying, “That’s enough, Alejandro, stop now,” and somehow my hand just keeps going back anyway.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I do know. That’s what bothers me. I’m standing there, perfectly aware that I’ve already had enough, and the sensible part of me is trying to put the brakes on, but I don’t seem to listen to it in the moment. That feeling of not being able to stop once I’ve started… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset before these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I’ve tried to think about that because it seems like there should be some clear reason before it happens, like I had a bad day or I’m worried about something, but I can’t always put my finger on it.\n\nSometimes I’m just… up, wandering around the house, and I end up in the kitchen. I wouldn’t say I always notice feeling anxious right beforehand. Sometimes I’ll end up going out to my workshop and sitting in that chair for a while, just sort of leaning back and waiting for my stomach to settle because lying down right away feels awful.\n\nAnd while I’m sitting there, I’m very aware that I went past the point where I should have stopped. It’s not just “oh, I had a snack.” It’s more like, “why did I keep going until I felt like this?” So yes, uncomfortably full is probably the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these nighttime eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Looking back, I’d say it’s been happening at least once most weeks. It’s not every single night, no, but it’s regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just an occasional thing anymore.\n\nA lot of weeks it’s more like two nights, especially since my cousin moved in. That seems to be when I started noticing it as a pattern, or maybe when it got more consistent. I’ll have a stretch where I think, “Okay, maybe I’ve got a handle on this,” and then another night comes along where I’m back in the kitchen with leftovers again. So… at least weekly, often twice in a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to stop eating once you've started during these episodes, or is it hard to stop even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the main part of it, honestly. It’s not just that I’m hungry and I eat something. It’s more like once I start picking at the leftovers late at night, something gets going and it’s hard to interrupt it.\n\nI’ll have this very clear thought in my head, like, “That’s enough, Alejandro, put it away.” I mean, I know what enough looks like. I’m not confused about that. But then my hand is already going back into the container, or I’m taking another forkful before I’ve really made a decision. It’s like the sensible part of me is standing there watching, telling me to stop, and the rest of me just keeps going anyway.\n\nSometimes I’ll even close the fridge or put the lid back on something, and then a minute later I’m opening it again. So yes, I do want to stop at a certain point, but in the moment it feels much harder than it should. It doesn’t feel like I’m fully in control of it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been tracking it closely enough to say, “Yes, this caused a certain amount of weight gain,” or anything like that.\n\nMy clothes fit a little differently at times, but at my age that can be from a lot of things, and I don’t want to pin it all on the nighttime eating if I don’t really know. I haven’t been getting on the scale regularly, so I’d be guessing.\n\nSo… significant changes? I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed anything major or definite. It’s more that I worry it could be affecting my weight, but I don’t have a clear number or trend to give you.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems with sexual performance or interest around the time these nighttime eating episodes started or since they've been happening?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really connected anything like that to the nighttime eating, no. I mean… at 65, some things in that area are not exactly the same as they were when I was younger, but I can’t say there was some clear change that started around the same time as the eating at night.\n\nI haven’t noticed a big drop in interest or a specific performance problem that made me think, “Oh, this is part of the same issue.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking closely, to be honest, but nothing stands out to me as a noticeable change since these episodes have been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in your interest in sex since these nighttime eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not in any clear way that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, like I said, I’m 65, so I don’t want to pretend my interest in sex is exactly what it was when I was 30 or 40. At least, I haven’t noticed a connection between the two.\n\nI mean, if you’re asking whether I eat at night because of something to do with sex, or because I’m frustrated that way, or whether the eating episodes make me less interested afterward… I can’t say that fits for me. It’s not something I’ve thought about as being linked. The nighttime eating feels like its own thing in my mind, and my interest in sex feels like… well, more separate, I guess.\n\nI suppose there could be things going on underneath that I’m not fully aware of, but from what I can tell, I haven’t noticed my sex drive changing when the nighttime eating is worse or better. Nothing like that has really stood out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have less interest in sex than you used to?\n\nPatient: Compared to when I was younger, yes, I suppose I probably do. I mean, I’m 65, so it would be hard for me to say I have the same level of interest I had decades ago. That wouldn’t really be true.\n\nBut I don’t know that I’d describe it as a big problem or some sudden drop-off. It’s more like… over time, life changes, your body changes, routines change. So yes, less than I used to, if we’re talking broadly. But not something I’ve been especially worried about or something that feels dramatic to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty immediately after one of these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “guilty” immediately, exactly. Then maybe later, if I think back on it, I might feel bad about it. But immediately after? I don’t know… not consistently. It depends on the night, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your lack of control during these nighttime eating episodes is more because of your emotions or because of physical hunger?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more complicated than just physical hunger, because a lot of the time I’m not actually hungry in the normal sense. I’ll have had a perfectly decent dinner with everyone, enough food, not skipping or anything like that, and then later at night I still end up in the kitchen.\n\nIt’s not usually like my stomach is growling and I need food. It’s more that I find myself opening containers, looking in the fridge, tasting a little of one thing and then another thing. Sometimes it almost starts as, “I’ll just have a bite,” and then I’m standing there sampling things even though I already ate. So if I had to choose, I’d say it doesn’t feel driven by true hunger most of the time.\n\nWhether that means it’s emotions, I don’t know exactly. I’m not always sitting there feeling very upset. But it definitely often happens when I’m not physically hungry, so the lack of control seems like it’s coming from somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your nighttime eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. I mean, sometimes I suppose if I’ve had a frustrating day or I’m feeling a bit restless, it might make it more likely, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern where I think, “Oh, I was upset, and that’s why it happened.”\n\nA lot of the time it feels more automatic than emotional, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be up, or wandering through the kitchen, or just sort of thinking about having a taste of something, and then it turns into more than I meant it to. It’s not that clear to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating at night because you feel like you have nothing better to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly.
